 1.21.3-5+deb13u1 deb CVE-2026-112323 High not-fixed The AUTH_GSSAPI handler in lib/rpc/svc_auth_gssapi.c in MIT Kerberos 5 (aka krb5) 1.5.2 and later allows remote attackers to cause a denial of service (memory exhaustion in kadmind) by sending repeated failed AUTH_GSSAPI_INIT requests. Each failed request allocates client state that is only freed after a 15-minute expiration timeout, allowing an attacker to accumulate memory faster than it is reclaimed.  8.14.1-2+deb13u7 deb CVE-2026-92388 Critical wont-fix When an FTP wildcard `LIST` operation fails, libcurl omits the restoration path meant for successful listings, leaving the write callback set to the internal listing parser and the write callback data pointing directly to the easy handle. Subsequent reuse of this handle triggers an immediate crash, and cloning it via `curl_easy_duphandle()` copies this corrupted state into the duplicate. If the original handle is subsequently freed, executing a transfer on the cloned handle causes a heap use-after-free read against the dangling handle reference; alternatively, reusing the original or cloned handle while changing only `CURLOPT_WRITEDATA` (and omitting `CURLOPT_WRITEFUNCTION`) results in type confusion, forcing the internal parser to interpret incoming server response bytes into memory at the application-supplied write data pointer.  5.40.1-6+deb13u1 deb CVE-2026-82560 High wont-fix Pod::Text versions before 6.1.1 for Perl allow CPU and memory exhaustion formatting a POD document whose =over nesting drives the margin to the output width. Each =over adds its indent to the margin, which wrap() subtracts from the output width to get the space available for text. When that space reaches zero, the line-splitting substitution matches the empty string, and the loop consumes no input while appending the margin padding on every pass. Formatting an attacker-supplied POD document never returns, and the output grows until memory is exhausted.   • /usr/share/doc/libssl3t64/copyright
  • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-124709 High not-fixed Issue summary: A QUIC server that rejects a stream opened by the peer may retain that stream's state for the lifetime of the connection if the peer resets the stream before the rejection is signalled, even though the peer's stream credit is returned. Impact summary: A remote client that completes a QUIC handshake can repeatedly open and reset streams, causing server memory to grow without bound until the process runs out of memory and every connection on it is lost. This is a Denial of Service. CWE: CWE-401: Missing Release of Memory after Effective Lifetime Description: QUIC servers track each stream the peer opens. When the incoming stream policy rejects a stream, OpenSSL schedules a STOP_SENDING frame, marks the stream deleted and returns the peer's stream credit. A deleted stream is collected only once its receive part is fully drained, which for a rejected stream means the STOP_SENDING has been acknowledged. If the peer sends RESET_STREAM first, the reset cancels the pending STOP_SENDING, so it is never sent or acknowledged and the stream is never collected. The default configuration reaches this path. Once a connection has a default stream, meaning the application has read from or written to the connection as the tutorial server does, the effective incoming stream policy rejects streams the application does not accept. Applications that set the REJECT policy explicitly are affected too. Each retained stream costs about 300 bytes, and the peer's stream credit window paces how quickly they accumulate. A client that keeps the connection open and keeps opening and resetting streams grows server memory without bound. The severity is Low because growth is slow, needs a long-lived connection, and causes no memory corruption or disclosure. FIPS impact: no The affected code is outside the FIPS module boundary. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

  • /var/lib/dpkg/status
  • /usr/share/doc/libssl3t64/copyright
  • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-95692 High not-fixed Issue summary: Setting an AES-GCM-SIV IV shorter than the expected nonce length via EVP_CipherInit_ex() may trigger a heap out-of-bounds read. Supplying a longer IV is silently truncated instead of rejected. Impact summary: An application passing a caller-supplied IV of the wrong length may crash or read beyond its buffer. Because trailing IV bytes are ignored, an attacker who knows one message and tag may forge messages under a different IV. CWE: CWE-125: Out-of-bounds Read Description: AES-GCM-SIV copies the caller-supplied IV or nonce into its internal state using a copy whose length is taken from the expected nonce size rather than from the length the caller actually provided. When the caller passes a shorter IV, the copy reads past the end of the caller's buffer; AddressSanitizer confirms a heap out-of-bounds read reached through EVP_CipherInit_ex() into ossl_aes_gcm_siv_init(). When the caller passes a longer IV, the implementation silently discards the trailing bytes instead of rejecting the length. The truncation breaks the binding between the IV and the computed authentication tag. An attacker who has observed a message and its tag under one IV can produce a message and tag that verify under a different IV whose first bytes match, since the surplus bytes never influence the computation. The message itself is unchanged, so this is a failure of IV commitment rather than a classic existential forgery. Only applications that call EVP_CipherInit_ex() (or the equivalent provider cipher initialization) with an IV length other than the standard 12 bytes for AES-GCM-SIV are affected. The severity is limited because the out-of-bounds read is small and attacker-controlled only in length, and the forgery requires an unusual IV length choice by the application. FIPS impact: no The FIPS modules are not affected by this issue, as AES-GCM-SIV is not an approved algorithm. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 8.14.1-2+deb13u7 deb CVE-2026-12064 High wont-fix When a user invokes curl using a schemeless URL combined with `--proto-default` sftp (or scp), a disconnect occurs between the tool layer and libcurl. The tool layer incorrectly infers the URL scheme, which erroneously bypasses the initialization of critical SSH security options like CURLOPT_SSH_HOST_PUBLIC_KEY_SHA256 and CURLOPT_SSH_KNOWNHOSTS. Conversely, the libcurl runtime successfully honors CURLOPT_DEFAULT_PROTOCOL and establishes the connection via SFTP/SCP as specified. Because the tool layer skipped the security configuration, these SSH host verification options are silently omitted, causing curl to connect to an unverified SSH remote host without throwing an error. [] pkg:deb/debian/curl@8.14.1-2%2Bdeb13u7?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7 N/A

 8.14.1-2+deb13u7 deb CVE-2026-82209 High wont-fix When libpsl support is enabled, libcurl fails to enforce the Public Suffix List boundary check when processing a `Set-Cookie` header where the `Domain` attribute explicitly matches an origin host that is itself a public suffix (e.g., `Domain=co.uk` set by `co.uk`). Instead of coercing it into a strict host-only cookie, libcurl saves the cookie with wildcard domain scope (`.co.uk`). Consequently, the cookie is inappropriately included in subsequent outbound requests or HTTP redirects to arbitrary sibling subdomains under the same public suffix (e.g., `attacker.co.uk`). [] pkg:deb/debian/curl@8.14.1-2%2Bdeb13u7?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7 N/A

 2.1.28+dfsg1-9 deb CVE-2026-107161 High wont-fix A heap-based buffer overflow flaw was found in Cyrus SASL. The add_to_challenge() function in the DIGEST-MD5 plugin computes the size of the buffer needed for a challenge/response field before DIGEST-MD5 quoting is applied, but does not recompute that size when quoting (escaping special characters) makes the value longer. The under-sized buffer is then passed to strcat(), causing a heap-based out-of-bounds write whose size depends on attacker-controlled input. A malicious or on-path DIGEST-MD5 (or HTTP Digest) server can trigger this flaw in a connecting client by supplying a crafted challenge field, such as realm or nonce, most likely resulting in a crash of the client application. [] pkg:deb/debian/libsasl2-2@2.1.28%2Bdfsg1-9?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=cyrus-sasl2 N/A

  • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-107496 High not-fixed Issue summary: Encrypting a CMS message to an EC certificate whose group order is 1 may cause CMS_encrypt() or CMS_encrypt_ex() to loop indefinitely. Impact summary: An attacker able to supply the recipient certificate to an application performing CMS encryption can cause that application to loop indefinitely, consuming CPU and resulting in Denial of Service. CWE: CWE-835: Loop with Unreachable Exit Condition ('Infinite Loop') Description: CMS_encrypt() and CMS_encrypt_ex() encrypt a message to one or more recipient certificates and, for an EC recipient, generate an ephemeral key in the recipient's group. If the certificate declares a group order of 1, random scalar generation can only produce zero. The key generation routine then retries without limit while waiting for a nonzero private scalar, so the call never returns. The OpenSSL TLS and QUIC implementations do not use this path. An application is exposed only if it calls CMS_encrypt() or CMS_encrypt_ex() with a recipient certificate whose EC parameters are attacker-controlled. Certificates with normal EC group orders do not trigger the loop. OpenSSL 4.0 and later reject unknown explicit EC groups in default builds. These versions are affected only when configured with the enable-ec_explicit_curves option. The result is a hang that consumes a CPU core until the process is interrupted, a Denial of Service. The severity is Low because the attacker must supply the recipient certificate and the application must choose to encrypt to it; ordinary CMS encryption to trusted recipients is unaffected. The fix makes EC key generation fail when the group has no valid nonzero private scalar, instead of retrying indefinitely. FIPS impact: no The FIPS modules in 4.1, 4.0, 3.5 and 3.0 are not affected. The FIPS provider does not support arbitrary explicit EC domain parameters, so an order-1 group is rejected during key import before the vulnerable key generation path is reached. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-124024 Medium not-fixed Converting crafted input from UCS-4 to IBM1390 or IBM1399 character encoding, in the GNU C Library version 2.3.3 and later may result in the converter reading past the end of the input buffer, causing the calling application to crash. When converting from UCS-4, these encoders may encounter a combining base such as U+00E6 and jump ahead by 4 bytes to check the next UCS-4 code point. The check for end of buffer however does not account for this, which may result in a buffer overread if the following code point has less than 4 bytes remaining. Exploitation of this bug not only requires attacker control of input and encoding, but also strict control over layout of the input in memory. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-19499 High wont-fix Calling strfmon and strfmon_l in the GNU C Library version 2.38 to 2.44 can write past the end of the caller-supplied output buffer when a conversion uses right-justified width padding. Exploitation requires an application code path that calls strfmon or strfmon_l with right-justified width padding into a destination buffer that is large enough for the padding to succeed but too small for the internal memmove call. The field width or format may be attacker-influenced or a fixed susceptible pattern in the caller. At the time of publication, no network-facing application impact is known. libssl3t64
  • /var/lib/dpkg/status
  • /usr/share/doc/libssl3t64/copyright
  • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-95691 High not-fixed Issue summary: A retransmitted QUIC CRYPTO frame can release packet storage while the TLS record layer retains a direct pointer into that storage causing use after free. Impact summary: A malicious QUIC server can cause a heap-use-after-free read in the OpenSSL QUIC client. The server can also identify one allocator-adjacent byte through the client's protected Handshake response. CWE: CWE-416: Use After Free Description: During CRYPTO stream processing, a pointer into the first frame's memory on the stream is lent to the TLS handshake processing. The memory must remain stable until it is processed and released. However the processing of retransmitted CRYPTO frames could release the memory if the retransmitted frame fully overlapped the existing frame. This then causes use-after-free read when the TLS handshake processing continues. The use-after-free read can lead to a Denial-of-Service of the client application or a minor leak of information about the heap memory contents that might be overwritten with other data, subsequently allocated by the application, to the peer. FIPS impact: no The FIPS module is not affected as the QUIC implementation is outside of the OpenSSL FIPS module boundary. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-84243 High wont-fix The gettext family of functions in the GNU C Library version 2.0 and later accept LANGUAGE environment variable entries that contain '/' or that are equal to "..", unless the program is setuid or setgid (AT_SECURE). An attacker who can set LANGUAGE in the environment of a program that uses gettext can make it load a message catalog (.mo file) from an arbitrary location in the file system. A crafted .mo file can crash the program through out-of-range offsets, or supply translations whose format directives do not match the arguments passed by the program. That causes undefined behaviour in the printf family of functions and may allow arbitrary code execution with the privileges of the program. The safety of message catalogs relies on them being installed in trusted, read-only locations, and the directory traversal bypasses this. The fix for CVE-2014-0475 rejects ".." path components in locale names passed to setlocale or taken from the LC_ALL, LC_* and LANG environment variables. gettext reads LANGUAGE directly and appends each colon-separated entry to the message catalog directory, so that fix did not cover LANGUAGE. LANGUAGE is only used when the LC_MESSAGES locale is not "C", "C.<encoding>" or "POSIX", so the attacker must also be able to select another installed locale, for example through LANG or LC_ALL. Exploitation also requires that the attacker can place the crafted file in a location readable by the target program, and that the environment crosses a trust boundary without the program running as AT_SECURE. An example is an SSH server that accepts LANGUAGE and LANG from clients through the AcceptEnv directive and restricts an account to a forced command; the attacker may use this to escape the restriction. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-84243 High wont-fix The gettext family of functions in the GNU C Library version 2.0 and later accept LANGUAGE environment variable entries that contain '/' or that are equal to "..", unless the program is setuid or setgid (AT_SECURE). An attacker who can set LANGUAGE in the environment of a program that uses gettext can make it load a message catalog (.mo file) from an arbitrary location in the file system. A crafted .mo file can crash the program through out-of-range offsets, or supply translations whose format directives do not match the arguments passed by the program. That causes undefined behaviour in the printf family of functions and may allow arbitrary code execution with the privileges of the program. The safety of message catalogs relies on them being installed in trusted, read-only locations, and the directory traversal bypasses this. The fix for CVE-2014-0475 rejects ".." path components in locale names passed to setlocale or taken from the LC_ALL, LC_* and LANG environment variables. gettext reads LANGUAGE directly and appends each colon-separated entry to the message catalog directory, so that fix did not cover LANGUAGE. LANGUAGE is only used when the LC_MESSAGES locale is not "C", "C.<encoding>" or "POSIX", so the attacker must also be able to select another installed locale, for example through LANG or LC_ALL. Exploitation also requires that the attacker can place the crafted file in a location readable by the target program, and that the environment crosses a trust boundary without the program running as AT_SECURE. An example is an SSH server that accepts LANGUAGE and LANG from clients through the AcceptEnv directive and restricts an account to a forced command; the attacker may use this to escape the restriction. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc6@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 8.14.1-2+deb13u7 deb CVE-2026-8458 Medium wont-fix libcurl might in some circumstances reuse the wrong connection when asked to do Negotiate-authenticated ones, even when they are set to use different "services". libcurl features a pool of recent connections so that subsequent requests can reuse an existing connection to avoid overhead. When reusing a connection a range of criteria must be met. Due to a logical error in the code, a request that was issued by an application could wrongfully reuse an existing connection to the same server that was authenticated using different services.  2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-6238 Medium wont-fix The deprecated functions ns_printrrf, ns_printrr and fp_nquery in the GNU C Library version 2.0.1 to version 2.43 fail to validate the RDATA content against the RDATA length in a DNS response when processing A6, CERT, LOC, TKEY or TSIG records, which may allow an attacker to craft a DNS response, causing a target application to crash or read uninitialized memory. These functions are for application debugging only and hence not in the path of code executed by the DNS resolver. Further, they have been deprecated since version 2.34 and should not be used by any new applications. Applications should consider porting away from these interfaces since they may be removed in future versions. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-121758 Medium not-fixed Converting crafted ISO-2022-CN-EXT input to UTF-8 character encoding, in the GNU C Library version 2.2 through 2.44 may result in the converter reading past the end of the input buffer, causing the calling application to crash. When the ISO-2022-CN-EXT decoder encounters an invalid input byte sequence like '\x0e' it ends up advancing the pointer tracking the read buffer position twice. If this sequence is at the end of the input buffer, the decoder may then end up reading past the bounds of the input buffer. To exploit this, the input and encoding needs to be attacker controlled.   • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-88823 High not-fixed Issue summary: The AES-GCM-SIV implementation treats a zero-length Additional Authenticated Data (AAD) update as a reset of all the AAD supplied so far, instead of leaving the AAD unchanged. An application that supplies its AAD in several fragments, one of which is empty, therefore produces or verifies messages whose authentication tag does not cover the AAD supplied before the empty fragment. Impact summary: Data an application intended to authenticate alongside a message is silently left unauthenticated. An attacker can forge the AAD of such a message. CWE: CWE-325: Missing Cryptographic Step Description: An application passing AAD in multiple chunks of which some have zero length will have the previous AAD chunks effectively unauthenticated. Also, on context re-initialisation, the AAD was not reset, which meant that any AAD accumulated from the previous context usage were also included in the AAD of the new message. The fix makes a zero-length AAD update a no-op and clears any accumulated AAD whenever the context is re-initialised with a key or a nonce. A re-initialisation that only changes parameters keeps the AAD. FIPS impact: no The FIPS modules are not affected, as AES-GCM-SIV is not a FIPS approved algorithm and is implemented only in the default provider, outside the OpenSSL FIPS module boundary. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-77117 Medium wont-fix Converting crafted SHIFT_JISX0213 input to UCS-4 or the internal wide character encoding, for example with iconv, in the GNU C Library version 2.3 to 2.44 may result in the converter making no progress, causing the calling application to hang. Some SHIFT_JISX0213 sequences decode to two code points. If the output buffer has room for only the first one, the converter stores the second in the conversion state and returns E2BIG, but it never clears that pending character after emitting it on the next call. The converter then keeps emitting the pending character without consuming further input, so an application that retries the conversion loops forever. The input must be attacker controlled and the application must convert it with an output buffer small enough to split the two code points. Only the SHIFT_JISX0213 character set is affected, which is not commonly used. The related defect in the EUC_JISX0213 converter is tracked separately as CVE-2026-80489. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u5 deb CVE-2026-80489 Medium wont-fix Converting crafted EUC_JISX0213 input to UCS-4 or the internal wide character encoding, for example with iconv, in the GNU C Library version 2.3 to 2.44 may result in the converter making no progress, causing the calling application to hang. Some EUC_JISX0213 sequences decode to two code points. If the output buffer has room for only the first one, the converter stores the second in the conversion state and returns E2BIG, but it never clears that pending character after emitting it on the next call. The converter then keeps emitting the pending character without consuming further input, so an application that retries the conversion loops forever. The input must be attacker controlled and the application must convert it with an output buffer small enough to split the two code points. Only the EUC_JISX0213 character set is affected, which is not commonly used. The related defect in SHIFT_JISX0213 converter is tracked separately as CVE-2026-77117. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u5?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 1:2.41.5-0+deb13u1 deb CVE-2026-78408 High wont-fix The nsenter --join-cgroup option opens the target cgroup.procs file as root and leaves that file descriptor open across later namespace and credential changes and across execve(). Because the kernel checks later cgroup migrations using the credentials from the original open, a program run in an attacker-controlled target can inherit root's ability to move host processes between cgroups. After a privileged operator uses --join-cgroup against that target, an unprivileged user can migrate and terminate unrelated root processes.  2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-121757 Medium not-fixed Converting crafted CP949 input to UTF-8 character encoding and ignoring invalid characters, in the GNU C Library version 2.1 or later may result in the converter reading past the end of the input buffer, causing the calling application to crash. When the CP949 decoder encounters an invalid input byte sequence like '\xa2\xe8' it ends up advancing the pointer tracking the read buffer position twice. If //IGNORE is set on the destination encoding and this sequence is at the end of the input buffer, the decoder may then end up reading past the bounds of the input buffer. To exploit this, the target iconv function needs to set //IGNORE on the destination of the conversion and the input and encoding needs to be attacker controlled. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-8674 Medium wont-fix Initializing the DNS stub resolver from an /etc/resolv.conf file, or a LOCALDOMAIN environment variable, whose search list contains a domain of roughly 200 characters or more in the GNU C Library version 2.26 to 2.44 results in an assertion failure which aborts the process. The resolver truncates the search list when copying it into the fixed-size _res.defdname buffer, then asserts that the copy is consistent with the full configuration. The consistency check compared against the wrong size and did not handle a first entry that does not fit, so a correctly truncated list failed the assertion. Any process that resolves names through the library is affected, including long-running processes that reload /etc/resolv.conf on the next query after it changes. Search domains are commonly written to /etc/resolv.conf from data received over DHCP or from a VPN server, so an attacker on the local network may be able to trigger this without privileges on the target system, subject to validation by the network configuration software. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-126765 Low not-fixed The glob functions in the GNU C Library versions 2.44 and earlier may free the same heap block twice when a memory allocation fails while glob prepends the directory name to the names it matched. The names already rewritten are freed, but their pointers stay in the result vector, and the error path frees them again with globfree. The allocation must fail after at least one name has been rewritten, so the issue can only be reached when the process is already close to its memory limit, for example under an address space limit (RLIMIT_AS) or on a 32-bit system. The attacker needs control to make the result large enough to make the program or service run out of memory, either by controlling the glob pattern and having knowledge of the filesystem layout or through other means. This issue affects all architectures and ABIs supported by the GNU C Library. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 1:2.41.5-0+deb13u1 deb CVE-2026-78410 High wont-fix A flaw was found in util-linux. Restricted bind mounts take the source path from fstab but do not pin that source before the privileged mount. A local unprivileged user who can replace the authorized source or a writable ancestor can redirect SUID mount(8) to bind another host directory. If the fstab entry also sets X-mount.owner, X-mount.group, or X-mount.mode, root then changes ownership or mode on that redirected inode. libssl3t64
  • /var/lib/dpkg/status
  • /usr/share/doc/libssl3t64/copyright
  • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-88822 Medium not-fixed Issue summary: QUIC, when performing automated peer detection, may allow uninitialized stack data to leak into the received address which is then used as the destination for outgoing packets. Impact summary: Applications using QUIC's automated peer detection mechanism may receive an invalid address, leading to connection loss and disclosure of uninitialized stack data to an attacker observing the outgoing network traffic. CWE: CWE-457 (Use of Uninitialized Variable) Description: The OpenSSL QUIC stack contains a feature in which, when using an already connected socket, it will auto-detect the peer address during the SSL handshake. This feature uses the getpeername() function, passing a buffer of sufficient length to hold any supported address family, but passes a buffer length that is only sized such that larger addresses, such as IPv6, cannot be retrieved. The getpeername() function however, still returns the full address length, which is accepted as the valid input buffer length, leading to the address buffer containing uninitialized stack data instead of the actual peer address. The invalid peer address is then used as destination for the outgoing packets which leads to a connectivity breakage and a leakage of the uninitialized stack data to passive network observers. FIPS impact: no The FIPS modules in 4.0, 3.6, 3.5, 3.4, and 3.0 are not affected by this CVE as the affected protocol is not implemented in the FIPS module. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 1:4.17.4-2 deb CVE-2024-56433 Low wont-fix shadow-utils (aka shadow) 4.4 through 4.17.0 establishes a default /etc/subuid behavior (e.g., uid 100000 through 165535 for the first user account) that can realistically conflict with the uids of users defined on locally administered networks, potentially leading to account takeover, e.g., by leveraging newuidmap for access to an NFS home directory (or same-host resources in the case of remote logins by these local network users). [] pkg:deb/debian/mount@2.41.5-0%2Bdeb13u1?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=util-linux N/A

 5.40.1-6+deb13u1 deb CVE-2026-15534 Medium wont-fix Perl versions through 5.45.1 have out-of-bounds heap reads and writes during regular expression matching via an undersized superlinear cache in S_regmatch. The regex engine's superlinear cache holds one bit per subject position for each participating WHILEM node, so the bit count is the subject length plus one times the number of nodes. Nothing checks that product for positive overflow of the signed 32-bit count: a 286331153 byte subject matched against a pattern with 15 participating nodes stores the count as 14, leaving a two byte cache. The cache is then indexed from the real match position and node number, so reads go past the end of the allocation, and on failure CACHEsayNO sets a bit past it. A caller that matches an attacker controlled subject of this size against a pattern of this shape can crash the process or corrupt heap memory. [] pkg:deb/debian/perl-base@5.40.1-6%2Bdeb13u1?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=perl N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-115819 Medium wont-fix Calling the wide scanf family of functions (swscanf, fwscanf, wscanf, vswscanf, vfwscanf and vwscanf) with a %s or %[ conversion without the 'l' length modifier in the GNU C Library version 2.2 to version 2.44 writes an extra null byte one past the end of the destination array when the converted multibyte string and its terminating null byte exactly fill that array. These conversions store each wide input character into the caller's char array as a multibyte sequence. The conversion ends with the sequence that returns to the initial shift state, which also stores the terminating null byte, and the functions then append a second null byte. A destination sized for the converted string and one terminator, as the C standard requires the caller to provide, is therefore written one byte past its end. The out-of-bounds byte is always a null byte, which may corrupt adjacent data or crash the process. An attacker who supplies the input can trigger the overflow with a string whose converted length exactly fills the destination. The narrow scanf functions, the %ls and %l[ conversions into a wchar_t array, and the 'a' and 'm' allocation modifiers are not affected, and storing a wide scanf conversion into a narrow char array is uncommon. This issue affects all architectures and ABIs supported by the GNU C Library. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-6791 Medium wont-fix When expanding paths that begin with a tilde (~) followed by a username, the internal parse_tilde function extracts the username to determine the user's home directory. The implementation allocates memory for this username directly on the stack using the strndupa macro. Because the size of this allocation was determined by the length of the user-supplied input without any bounds checks, passing an excessively long username e.g. thousands of characters, forces the thread to exhaust its stack space. Thus if an application passes untrusted, attacker-controlled input to the wordexp function, an attacker can trigger a stack clash. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-19542 Medium wont-fix Calling tdelete on a sufficiently deep tree in the GNU C Library version 2.1 to 2.44 may write one pointer past the end of an alloca-allocated array on the stack, which may crash the application. The tdelete implementation keeps an explicit stack of parent nodes for rebalancing, which is grown as needed while descending the tree. Two rebalancing branches push an additional entry without checking the capacity, and write past the array when the stack is exactly full. Triggering this requires a node at a depth of exactly 40 (or 40 plus a multiple of 20), which implies a tree with at least a million nodes, so an attacker must drive a large number of insertions and deletions through an application that uses tsearch and tdelete. The written value is a pointer into a tree node and is not directly attacker controlled. No affected application in common distributions has been identified. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

Two rebalancing branches push an additional entry without checking the capacity, and write past the array when the stack is exactly full. Triggering this requires a node at a depth of exactly 40 (or 40 plus a multiple of 20), which implies a tree with at least a million nodes, so an attacker must drive a large number of insertions and deletions through an application that uses tsearch and tdelete. The written value is a pointer into a tree node and is not directly attacker controlled. No affected application in common distributions has been identified. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc6@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u5?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-100290 Low wont-fix The regcomp and regexec functions in the GNU C Library version 2.3 to version 2.44 may read freed memory and free the same heap block twice when a memory allocation fails while a regular expression is compiled or matched. The re_dfa_add_node (the NFA node table) and build_range_exp (bracket ranges) grow several arrays with consecutive realloc calls and store the new pointers only after all of them succeed. When a later call fails, the pattern buffer keeps pointers to blocks that were already freed, and the error cleanup frees them again. In regexec, the back-reference cache (match_ctx_add_entry) frees its array when a realloc fails but keeps the pointer, which is freed again on the error path. An attacker does not need to supply a crafted regular expression: patterns large enough to grow these arrays are common. The failure has to hit a specific allocation, though, so the issue can only be reached when the system is already under memory pressure. An attacker who supplies the pattern or the subject string has little control over which allocation fails, so the expected impact is a crash of the process. This issue affects all architectures and ABIs supported by the GNU C Library. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-89092 Medium wont-fix The nscd service in the GNU C Library 2.3.4 onwards may crash due to a stack overflow when a malicious DNS server returns too large a response for a DNS query, resulting in degraded DNS resolution for the system. Exploitation of this bug needs a system that has nscd enabled and using an untrusted DNS server for name resolution, with the compromised DNS server being capable of processing records large enough to result in a stack overflow in an nscd thread stack.  During experimentation, bind 9 was unable to handle large records, but that could change in future or with a different name server.  In typical installations, nscd is executed in an isolated context as its own user without a shell, due to which any compromise of that service is isolated. There is a remote possibility of nscd cache corruption if an attacker manages to get the stack pointer into a desired point in the heap, potentially resulting in other caches in nscd being overwritten with corrupt data through the stack overflow, until the buggy code path eventually results in a crash. Finally, a crash in nscd may result in performance degradation when resolving names, but it does not result in a denial of service. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

  • /var/lib/dpkg/info/libssl3t64:amd64.md5sums
 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-107493 High not-fixed Issue summary: When an application passes a stack to PEM_X509_INFO_read_bio_ex() to add entries to, and reading fails, OpenSSL frees every entry in the stack, both the old ones and any new ones, but leaves them in the stack. Impact summary: An application that passes an existing stack to PEM_X509_INFO_read_bio_ex() and keeps using it after a failed read may use memory that was already freed. This can cause a crash or, in principle, memory corruption. Malformed PEM data can make the read fail, but only applications that pass an existing stack are affected, and OpenSSL itself does not do this. CWE: CWE-416: Use After Free Description: PEM_X509_INFO_read_bio_ex() reads certificates, CRLs and private keys from PEM data and adds them to a stack of X509_INFO structures. If the application passes a stack that is not NULL, the function adds the new entries to it and must leave the entries that are already there unchanged. If reading fails, the function frees every entry in the stack. This includes the entries that were there before the call, and the entries added by the call. It does not remove these entries from the stack, so the stack keeps pointers to freed memory. The failure can happen at any point, even before any entry was added. A PEM item with invalid content is enough. This also happens if the stack was empty: entries added before the failure are freed but stay in the stack. An application that then reads or frees the stack will use memory that was already freed. This can crash the application or, in principle, corrupt memory. Only applications that pass a stack that is not NULL are affected. OpenSSL itself never does this. Its own callers, including the code that TLS uses to load CA certificates, always pass NULL. When the stack is NULL, the function frees only the entries that it created itself, which is safe. We expect that very few applications pass an existing stack. An attacker can make the read fail with malformed PEM data, but cannot make an application pass an existing stack. The same problem affects PEM_X509_INFO_read_bio(), PEM_X509_INFO_read() and PEM_X509_INFO_read_ex(), which use the same code. OpenSSL 1.1.1 and 1.0.2 have only the first two of these functions. FIPS impact: no The affected code is outside the FIPS module boundary. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

CWE: CWE-416: Use After Free Description: PEM_X509_INFO_read_bio_ex() reads certificates, CRLs and private keys from PEM data and adds them to a stack of X509_INFO structures. If the application passes a stack that is not NULL, the function adds the new entries to it and must leave the entries that are already there unchanged. If reading fails, the function frees every entry in the stack. This includes the entries that were there before the call, and the entries added by the call. It does not remove these entries from the stack, so the stack keeps pointers to freed memory. The failure can happen at any point, even before any entry was added. A PEM item with invalid content is enough. This also happens if the stack was empty: entries added before the failure are freed but stay in the stack. An application that then reads or frees the stack will use memory that was already freed. This can crash the application or, in principle, corrupt memory. Only applications that pass a stack that is not NULL are affected. OpenSSL itself never does this. Its own callers, including the code that TLS uses to load CA certificates, always pass NULL. When the stack is NULL, the function frees only the entries that it created itself, which is safe. We expect that very few applications pass an existing stack. An attacker can make the read fail with malformed PEM data, but cannot make an application pass an existing stack. The same problem affects PEM_X509_INFO_read_bio(), PEM_X509_INFO_read() and PEM_X509_INFO_read_ex(), which use the same code. OpenSSL 1.1.1 and 1.0.2 have only the first two of these functions. FIPS impact: no The affected code is outside the FIPS module boundary. [] pkg:deb/debian/openssl-provider-legacy@3.5.9-1~deb13u1?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 3.5.7-1~deb13u3 deb CVE-2026-35190 Medium not-fixed Issue summary: A server configured with the Suite B certificate flag through SSL_CTX_set_cert_flags() and with TLS 1.3 enabled may read past the end of a fixed group list when it processes a ClientHello whose supported_groups and key_share contain no Suite B group. Impact summary: A remote client can send a ClientHello that makes the server read past the end of a static array. The values read are never returned to the client, so no memory is disclosed; on builds with sanitizers or hardened allocators the read may crash the process, causing Denial of Service. CWE: CWE-125: Out-of-bounds Read Description: When the Suite B certificate flag is set, OpenSSL restricts the accepted groups to P-256 and P-384 by substituting a fixed two-element list for the configured group list. The tuple machinery used for key-share selection was not updated for this substitution: tls1_get_group_tuples() still returns the configured tuples, which by default describe eight groups. The server-side walk in tls_parse_ctos_key_share() then advances through the two-element list using those tuple counts without checking the cumulative offset against the number of groups present. Only applications that call SSL_CTX_set_cert_flags() with SSL_CERT_FLAG_SUITEB_128_LOS and leave TLS 1.3 enabled are affected. The openssl s_server -suiteB_128 option sets an X.509 verification parameter instead, so the stock command line server is not vulnerable. Default configurations do not reach this code. A client that offers only groups outside the Suite B set, such as X25519, forces every tuple to be walked and the read to run past the array. The bytes read are compared against client group IDs and never sent back, so no memory is disclosed. On builds with address sanitizers or hardened allocators the read can abort the process; in ordinary release builds it reads a few bytes of adjacent constant data. FIPS impact: no The affected code is outside the FIPS module boundary. [] pkg:deb/debian/libssl3t64@3.5.7-1~deb13u3?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=openssl N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-86805 Medium wont-fix A time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) race condition in the dynamic loader (ld.so) of the GNU C Library (glibc) versions 2.14 through 2.44 allows a local attacker to escalate privileges. When expanding $ORIGIN in DT_RPATH for setuid/setgid (AT_SECURE) programs, glibc validates the lexically normalized search path against the trusted directories but then opens the raw, un-normalized path. On systems where the Linux fs.protected_hardlinks sysctl is disabled, a local attacker who hard-links such a program into an attacker-controlled directory and wins a race to replace an intermediate path component with a symbolic link can direct the loader outside the trusted directory, causing it to load an attacker-controlled shared object and execute arbitrary code with the elevated privileges of the program. Exploitation requires an installed setuid or setgid binary whose DT_RPATH uses $ORIGIN followed by ".." traversal that normalizes into a trusted directory, and the ability to hard-link that binary and win the race by swapping a path component for a symbolic link. Major Linux-based OS distributions ship with fs.protected_hardlinks enabled by default and mitigate the vulnerability. [] pkg:deb/debian/libc-bin@2.41-12%2Bdeb13u4?arch=amd64&distro=debian-13.7&upstream=glibc N/A

 2.41-12+deb13u4 deb CVE-2026-95818 Low wont-fix A stack-based buffer overflow in the dynamic loader (ld.so) of the GNU C Library (glibc) versions 2.14 through 2.44 allows a local attacker to crash or corrupt the memory of setuid/setgid (AT_SECURE) programs. The corrupted loader stack can lead to a loader crash (denial of service) and limited disclosure of process memory.
